# Japan Insight: BofA Strategy Monthly

# Reassessing Japan equities in the age of AI, hardware and physical assets

Investment Strategy

# Reversal in merits of industrial structure: Hardware and physical assets now hold an advantage

Although guidance this results season was conservative, in such years guidance tends to rise in a stepwise fashion as 1Q and 2Q results are released. However, the risk is that inflation pushes up interest rates, but in the US, this is in line with stronger-than-expected economic data. Domestic interest rates have risen on concerns about fiscal deterioration, and 10y JGB yields have temporarily risen to 2.8%, but the TOPIX earnings yield is c.6%. On the other hand, a positive scenario is that rising US long-term interest rates force President Trump to soften his stance, leading to the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz.

It is also important to note that the medium-term attractiveness of Japanese equities is increasing. Japanese companies provide semiconductor manufacturing equipment (SPE), components and materials that are essential to the AI supply chain. In addition, supply chains and production facilities are being rebuilt globally to mitigate geopolitical threats, and wealth is concentrated in these areas. Now is the time to reassess Japan's industrial structure, which has strengths in hardware and physical assets that are easy to pass on to prices. Previous catalysts, such as the shift to an inflationary economy and the acceleration of corporate reform, are also alive and well.

# Initial AI rally has passed, but uptrend likely to continue

From a stock-selection perspective, we believe it is time to focus on AI stocks while diversifying across sectors. The initial rally in AI market has passed, but the uptrend is likely to continue. If the Strait of Hormuz is reopened, high-beta AI stocks have upside potential in a phase where only expectations are rising, and stocks that benefit from fundamentals have upside potential after the Strait is actually reopened. As a basic view, we believe sectors that were the main preference of investors before the closure of the Strait of Hormuz should be included in portfolios. If gradual yen appreciation emerges, there may be opportunities to re-evaluate consumer-related sectors that have historically been disadvantaged.

# Our economists, FX/rates strategists and sector analysts discuss their outlooks

In this report, our economists, forex/rates strategists and sector analysts discuss their respective outlooks. Our sector analysts have recommended stocks to watch, and selected 10 stocks that also align with the top-down perspective.

# BofA's 10 highlighted names (from top-down and bottom-up approaches)

We selected 10 stocks that match our top-down view as strategists from among the stocks selected by analysts from a bottom-up perspective. We assume an investment period of the next three months. Our selection focuses on component and materials companies and companies that are transforming into AI stocks, in light of the broadening scope of the AI market. For domestic demand sectors, we selected stocks with global exposure and high earnings defensiveness, given the closure of the Strait of Hormuz. (Chief Japan Equity Strategist Masashi Akutsu is responsible for the content).

Kirin Holdings (2503): 1Q business profit beat consensus. We believe the company can absorb the impact of the Middle East conflict through cost reductions and price hikes, which is reassuring.

Tokyo Ohka Kogyo (4186): Earnings momentum remains strong, supported by AI-related demand. 1Q results suggest that the FY27/12 medium-term target for operating profit could be achieved as early as this fiscal year. An upward revision in 2Q, as well as a significant upward revision to the medium-term targets thereafter, is likely to come into focus. With its expanding market capitalization, the stock appears to have become more widely favored by overseas investors.

Maruwa (5344): We take into account the broadening of AI stock selection. Guidance excessively factors in an impact from the Middle East conflict, but we see significant upside potential in the AI and semiconductor businesses. Earlier-than-expected shipments of ceramic heat dissipation substrates for CPOs in 2H would be an additional upside factor.

Nichias (5393): We take into account the broadening of AI stock selection. Materials for SPE are growing. In addition, the company has many catalysts, including increased demand for maintenance owing to the restart of nuclear power plants and augmented shareholder returns.

Recruit Holdings (6098): Perceptions of the company changed after full-year results beat the consensus. Results confirmed AI premiumization of unit prices in HR Tech. It is encouraging that management targets a 41% EBITDA margin in FY3/27 and 50% over the medium term.

NEC (6701): The P/Es of software companies have declined globally on concerns about AI disruption, but we believe domestic IT service companies can achieve growth in EPS. The company's five-year medium-term plan targets a 15% CAGR in profit growth, which is ambitious.

Panasonic (6752): In addition to the previously identified catalyst of structural reform, new drivers such as growth in data center-related BBU have emerged.

Renesas Electronics (6723): The company is redefining its data center-related businesses, and its valuation may be given an AI premium in the future. The recovery in automotive semiconductor prices is also a positive factor.

Asics (7936): We believe the company, which has a growth path overseas, is likely to attract investor funds.

Mizuho Financial Group (8411): Bank stocks are (1) a hedge against rising interest rates owing to concerns about higher prices and fiscal deterioration, and (2) could also benefit if the Strait of Hormuz is reopened and a decline in fiscal concerns lead to less government pressure on the BoJ to raise rates. Guidance is conservative for assuming no BoJ rate hikes, and we see significant upside potential for ROE in the medium term.

# Sector recommendations

Below is our investment thesis for each sector as strategists. These investment theses are based on analyst views, earnings cycles, valuations and top-down scenarios (investment horizon of three months). We are Overweight on AI-related sectors; the industrial electronics and machinery sectors in light of the favorable environment for hardware and recovery in manufacturing; banks and financial services based on their earnings resilience and a hedge against rising interest rates; and trading companies considering elevated commodity prices and trading opportunities from the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz. The closure of the Strait of Hormuz is a negative for the construction sector, but we take into account its relative earnings stability.

We are Underweight on sectors with heavy labor and logistics costs and weak profit growth amid inflation. The automobile sector could benefit from the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz, but we also consider the possibility of yen appreciation and the lingering tariff burden. We plan to consider raising our weighting once consensus estimates have come down sufficiently. We shift our stance on IT services to Neutral, as we believe EPS growth is possible amid adjustments to P/E multiples.

# Equity strategy (Masashi Akutsu / Tetsuhiro Tokuyama)

# Strong earnings, but guidance is conservative

Japanese equities have surged since April, even as the Strait of Hormuz effectively remains closed. AI stocks were the main driver of the rally. A pullback from the rapid rise is naturally possible, but the increase in share prices is natural. This rally is the result of the global dominance of AI, hardware and real assets, and the superiority of Japan's industrial structure. We believe Japanese equities are headed upward over the medium term.

Here, we summarize recent developments. First, announcements of full-year results, which have run their course, have set new record highs for profits, and guidance also calls for profit growth (see our 18 May Japan Equity Strategy). Looking at the contribution to guidance by sector, the electrical equipment sector where many AI stocks belong stands out in the manufacturing industry (Exhibit 3). In the non-manufacturing industry, sectors heavily affected by the Strait of Hormuz closure have seen a decline in profits, but profit growth in the banking sector has offset this to a considerable extent (Exhibit 4). Profit growth in AI and banks is supporting overall earnings.

On the other hand, guidance is more conservative than usual (Exhibit 5). In years when guidance is conservative, the typical pattern is (1) sluggish share price performance until full-year results, (2) a rebound after results, but then a sideways movement due to a softening in analyst forecasts in response to conservative guidance, and (3) a stepwise rise in share prices after 1Q and 2Q results come out (Exhibit 6). We expect TOPIX to rise another notch and then hold steady, but we see upside potential toward the end of the year. Meanwhile, the Nikkei 225, where AI stocks contribute considerably to the index, may continue to rise.

# Risk is rising interest rates; positive risk is reopening of Strait of Hormuz

So, what are the risks? Recently, the sharp rise in interest rates has become a threat to the market. AI stocks with strong EPS growth have also shown a vulnerable reaction to rising interest rates (their P/E multiples have declined). In particular, US inflation needs to be monitored. This is because the impact of higher gasoline prices and also the AI investment boom itself has started to push up goods inflation. In fact, it is unusual for the PCE deflator to start rising faster than the CPI. This may be owing to the high weighting of IT equipment in the former (Exhibit 7). If inflation is being caused by supply factors, such as immigration restrictions and rising gasoline prices, it is reasonable for the Fed to keep the policy rate unchanged. If inflation is due to demand factors, including AI investment, a rate hike will be necessary.

This is not a problem that can be quickly fixed. It is too early to fully price in Fed rate hikes, as long-term expectations for inflation, which Fed Chair Kevin Warsh watches closely, are also stable. As long as this is the case, the rise in US interest rates is based on the upside in economic statistics, including the rate of inflation, and it is not at a stage where it will have a significant impact on the market (Exhibit 8). Interest rates rose sharply in March amid weakening economic statistics, but recently the pace of overshooting in economic statistics (and improvement in US corporate EPS) appears to be faster (Exhibit 9).

The rise in domestic interest rates is more rooted in concerns about fiscal deterioration. The pace of interest rate increases has begun to outpace growth in EPS, and if this continues, it will likely affect stocks. However, Prime Minister Takaichi stated that the supplementary budget would be kept within a level that does not increase bond issuance. Although the 10-year JGB yield briefly rose to 2.8%, it is not a major problem because the nominal GDP growth rate has stayed around 4%. The TOPIX earnings yield is also around 6% (Exhibit 10).

It is true that many observers take a negative view of the fact that the 10-year JGB yield has exceeded the dividend yield. However, the high earnings yield is due to accelerating EPS growth under inflation, and companies are also conducting large share buybacks. It is not reasonable to compare only interest rates and dividend yields as in deflationary periods.

On the positive risk side, it is time to consider a scenario where the Strait of Hormuz is reopened. US-Iran negotiations have started to progress, and given the recent changes on the Trump side, some form of agreement may be possible. The first reason is the sharp rise in US long-term interest rates. When tariffs were imposed last year, fluctuations in long-term interest rates, rather than stocks, forced President Trump to change his stance. Second, President Trump's approval rating has fallen below 40%, and support for his policies on inflation has fallen below 30% (Exhibit 12). If high gasoline prices persist in the US, which entered the driving season in late May, their impact on the November midterm elections will likely be significant.

# Reversal in merits of industrial structure: Hardware and physical assets now at an advantage

In summary, while there are both positive and negative factors at work in the near-term market, there is room for upside toward the end of the year, considering trends in earnings. It is also important to note that the medium-term attractiveness of Japanese equities is increasing.

The emerging bullish view on Japanese equities is that Japan's industrial structure is becoming increasingly advantageous. Japanese companies provide semiconductor manufacturing equipment (SPE), components and materials essential to the AI supply chain. In addition, supply chains and production facilities are being rebuilt globally to mitigate geopolitical threats, and demand is concentrated in these areas. Compared to the 2010s, when supply chains were optimized to the extreme, the current situation is more inflationary, with prices rising in fields where demand is concentrated.

Hardware and physical assets, which can easily pass on higher costs, are at an advantage, while software and intangible assets, which see little fluctuations in prices and are exposed to the risk of AI disruption, are at a disadvantage. From another perspective, profit growth is more pronounced in B2B than in B2C in an inflationary economy. In fact, since the shift to inflation in Japan, profit growth has strengthened in industrials, IT (which has a lot of AI hardware companies), and sectors that benefit greatly from inflation. Software, intangible assets and consumer-related sectors are less attractive (Exhibit 13, Exhibit 14). Japan has a high weighting of hardware in its industrial structure, and the disadvantages of this, seen in the 2010s, may be reversing.

# Structural changes point to gradual yen appreciation

In terms of forex, we believe the aforementioned industrial structure will ultimately lead to a gradual appreciation of the yen. Our forex strategists have revised their medium-term view of yen depreciation to neutral (estimating ¥152/\$ at end-2026 and ¥145/\$ at end-2027). (The following discussion is based on our 19 May FX Viewpoint). The past supply-demand structure suggested that the yen would depreciate if left alone, but the expansion of exports of Al-related goods has begun to offset Japan's digital deficit (Exhibit 15).

Inward direct investment has also increased in response to changes in the geopolitical landscape, offsetting the persistent increase in outward direct investment (Exhibit 16). Individual investors' purchases of overseas assets have also been a structural factor in yen selling, but interest in Japanese equities is growing (see above report). Ultimately, a Japan trade of buying Japanese equities and buying the yen may materialize.

# Structural catalysts remain intact

In addition, the traditional catalysts for Japanese equities remain intact. The first catalyst is the structural shift from a deflationary to an inflationary economy. The rally in Japanese equities so far can largely be explained by the upside potential in nominal growth rates accompanying the shift to inflation (Exhibit 17). The Takaichi administration's policies also prioritize aggressive investment in growth fields and the expansion of macro supply capacity and the nominal economic pie itself.

A second catalyst is the acceleration in corporate reforms. Japanese companies have begun to use their vast amounts of cash accumulated during the deflationary period (Exhibit 19). Since the pivot to inflation in 2023, companies with low net cash, i.e., those that are making effective use of their cash, have been rewarded (Exhibit 20). Activist pressure and the revised Corporate Governance Code, which is to be implemented by summer, should support this trend.

As a result, TOPIX ROE now exceeds 10% (Exhibit 21). TOPIX P/E has exceeded the previous upper end of its range of 16x, but the trigger was ROE breaking out of its range. This may suggest an upward shift in the P/E range. As we mentioned above, if yen depreciation reverses course, it will contribute to the increase in ROE by curbing growth in foreign currency translation adjustment accounts (Exhibit 22). As a result of the unwinding of cross-shareholdings, increases in unrealized gains on other securities have already been curbed (Exhibit 23).

With these structural changes, foreign investors are accelerating their purchases of Japanese equities. In particular, we note a shift in funds by North American investors, who had previously been the slowest to move (Exhibit 24). We believe European and US investors are still underweight Japanese equities, and that Japanese equities have significant potential upside. Based on the above, we raise our year-end forecasts for Japanese equities (TOPIX: from 4,100 to 4,200, Nikkei 225: from 61,000 to 67,000).

# Initial rally in AI market has passed, but uptrend likely to continue

Finally, we summarize our views on stock selection. We believe it is time to diversify across sectors, while keeping the focus on AI stocks. AI stocks have surged, and while it is difficult to compare AI to the past because it is an unprecedented technology, all AI stocks have high betas. Looking at high-beta stock rallies since 2003, we see these commonalities: (1) a long-term upward trajectory, but (2) an expiration date for the initial rally (Exhibit 25-Exhibit 31). Taking (2) into account, the initial surge in AI stocks may be nearing over.

We cannot rule out a short-term overheating in AI stocks. When we divide stocks into quintiles based on their betas and measure their relative performance, only the highest beta quintile outperforms (Exhibit 32). A similar pattern occurred in September-October 2025, but a return reversal materialized in early November as expectations receded for Fed rate cuts. At that time, the correction ended when the N/T ratio (Nikkei divided by

TOPIX) plunged from +3σ to +1σ above the average (Exhibit 33). This time, the N/T ratio also rose to +3σ above the average at one point, and if a similar correction occurs, the correction is likely to end at around 15.5x, which is +1σ above the average N/T ratio.

On the other hand, the AI market itself is likely to last for a long time. Compared to the dot-com bubble around 2000, the market is still only in the early stages of an upward trend, even if it is likely to stall in the near term (Exhibit 34, Exhibit 35). The recovery in the manufacturing industry since the Fed cut rates last year is similar to the developments after the 1998 rate cut, which was one of the contributing factors to the dot-com bubble (Exhibit 36). Back then, the Fed's consecutive rate hikes triggered the collapse of the bubble, but no rate hikes have been implemented yet (Exhibit 37). In this context, we believe that sector diversification is necessary, but exiting AI stocks prematurely should also be avoided.

# Highlighted Sectors

What would happen if the Strait of Hormuz were reopened? First, in a phase where only expectations rise, high-beta AI stocks are likely to rise reflexively. High-beta stocks are most likely to react when oil prices, market volatility and interest rates fall (Exhibit 38).

If the Strait of Hormuz is actually reopened, the impact on fundamentals must also be considered. A fall in raw material prices are likely to lead to a sharp rise in sectors such as transportation equipment, rubber products, chemicals and steel. However, most of these sectors were not favored by investors, even before the closure of the Strait of Hormuz. We believe that sectors that were favored before the closure of the Strait of Hormuz should be included in portfolios.

Specifically, these sectors are (1) construction, which benefits from falling material prices; (2) real estate, which benefits from a decline in the need for gasoline subsidies and the loss of the fiscal premium (decline in interest rates); (3) Takaichi stocks, including defense, if the government's financial resources and policy priorities shift from Hormuz measures to growth strategies; and (4) banks, if reduced fiscal concerns lead to less government pressure on the BoJ to raise interest rates.

Among these, banks are an effective hedge against rising interest rates, as their full-year guidance is stronger than expected. We also maintain our Overweight stances on machinery, energy infrastructure-related industrial machinery, and trading companies (which benefit from rising resource prices and increased trading opportunities if the Strait of Hormuz is reopened), which we have favored in the past.

Looking ahead, if the yen gradually strengthens after the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz, as we mentioned above, stock selection may also broaden to consumer-related sectors such as railroads, airlines, retail, services, food, and cosmetics/toiletries (Exhibit 39). If inflation slows, defensive sectors such as pharmaceuticals and healthcare may also see their undervaluation corrected (Exhibit 40).

At this stage, stocks with global exposure and unique growth potential in these sectors rank highly in our order of preference. The reopening of the Strait of Hormuz may provide an opportunity to reassess stocks with significant domestic exposure. IT service companies, which have seen a significant adjustment in their P/E multiples, are unlikely to return to their previous levels, but we see upside potential in line with EPS recoveries after quarterly earnings are confirmed.

From a stock screening perspective, we have traditionally favored high-beta/high-earnings stocks (Exhibit 41). This includes many AI stocks. From a sector diversification perspective, stocks with solid full-year results, as shown in Exhibit 42, may also be a useful reference. Many share buybacks were announced along with the full-year results, which will support share prices once they enter the execution stage. Exhibit 43 lists the stocks that announced share buybacks, with its announced amount against to market cap of at least 0.5%.

BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH

# Japan economy (Izumi Devalier / Takayasu Kudo)

# Growth: Gradual slowdown as Middle East spillovers become more visible

Preliminary 1Q CY26 real GDP showed solid growth of 0.5% QoQ SA (+2.1% annualized), with broad-based expansion across expenditure components (Exhibit 44; see Jan-Mar GDP (Prelim): Broad-based expansion; downside risks building 19 May 2026). Domestic consumption, supported by rising real wages (see Wages update: Near-term resilience amid elevated Middle East uncertainty 11 May 2026), firm capex, and export growth (including AI-related products) all lifted activity.

That said, spillovers from Middle East disruptions are becoming more visible. Since March, both business and consumer sentiment have deteriorated sharply, and resource supply shortages have led to a sizable drop in upstream petrochemical production (see Chartbook (Apr '26): Hormuz disruptions start to bite 15 May 2026). While the government aims to secure stable supplies of crude oil and petroleum products, as the impact spreads beyond upstream sectors, we expect GDP growth to slow gradually from 2H CY26.

# Inflation: Near-term disinflation; re-acceleration from this autumn

Core CPI momentum has continued to slow, reflecting policy effects and negative base effects from last year's sharp food-price increases (Exhibit 45). With the government subsidies capping energy costs and FY-start price revisions remaining modest, the disinflation trend is likely to persist near-term (see April CPI: Downside surprise, with subdued price hikes at the start of the fiscal year 01 May 2026).

By contrast, April PPI points to a sharp rise in prices for naphtha and petrochemical intermediates. While pass-through to consumer prices will take time, additional cost pressures raise the likelihood of CPI re-acceleration from this autumn. Medium- to long-term inflation expectations—an important input into the BoJ’s assessment of “underlying inflation”—are also likely to rise further (see Inflation expectations are likely to rise further 20 April 2026).

BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH

# Fiscal policy: A steady run of headlines through May–June

The government is cushioning the impact of higher crude oil prices through energy subsidies, at the cost of rising fiscal burdens. In line with market expectations, it has begun considering a first supplementary budget for FY26 to secure funding for these measures. We expect a ¥3–5tn package to be compiled around June–July (see Cost of energy subsidies: rising risk of a supplementary budget ahead of summer 23 April 2026).

Beyond the supplementary budget, the government will publish its Honebuto (Basic Policy) guidelines in June. The guidelines are likely to cover household transfers—potentially including measures such as a food consumption tax cut—as well as a new fiscal framework, both of which could shape the medium- to long-term fiscal path (see Japan’s fiscal policy: A guide to the debate 18 May 2026).

In addition, the Liberal Democratic Party (LDP) proposals on “Three Strategic Documents,” also due in June, are likely to signal a direction toward higher defense spending from the current 2% of GDP (see Japan at a crossroad: Defense spending, security imperatives and fiscal limits 02 April 2026).

# Monetary policy: The BoJ is increasingly vigilant on upside price risks

At the 28 April Monetary Policy Meeting, the Bank of Japan (BoJ) left the policy rate unchanged at $0.75\%$ . However, the statement, Outlook Report, and Governor Ueda's press conference were more hawkish than expected (see BoJ review: A hawkish hold 28 April 2026). The BoJ is becoming increasingly concerned about upside price risks stemming from developments in the Middle East. While it stayed on hold in April given elevated uncertainty, we think the bank will proceed with a hike in June as the outlook for Middle East tensions becomes clearer.

Subsequent materials—including the Summary of Opinions and Policy Board speeches—carried a similarly hawkish tone (see BoJ Watch: Apr MPM SOP: Hawkish tone as expected 12 May 2026). Hence, we expect the BoJ to raise the policy interest rate to 1.0% at the next 15–16 June meeting, unless (1) tensions in the Middle East escalate further or (2) significant financial market disruptions occur around the June meeting.

Thereafter, we continue to expect 25bp hikes in October 2026, March 2027, and July 2027, maintaining our terminal rate forecast at 1.75% at end-2027. That said, if the upside price risks highlighted by the BoJ materialize, the pace of hikes could be faster and/or the terminal rate higher than 1.75%.

# JPY: Wake up, yen... a window may be opening (Shusuke Yamada)

Full report: FX Viewpoint: Wake up, yen... a window may be opening 19 May 2026

# Medium-term JPY view revised toward neutral

We have been structurally bearish on JPY since 2021, and since 2022 have premised our outlook on a prolonged period of JPY weakness driven by structural outflows. However, there are now signs of an improvement in structural flow dynamics. Moreover, other major currencies face their own risk factors. As a result, the medium-term outlook for JPY has improved. That said, a clear JPY long would still require a policy shift—or FX and rate levels that would trigger such a shift—as well as a decline in oil prices.

# A shift in one of the two drivers of JPY weakness

There have been two key drivers behind JPY depreciation in recent years. First, persistent structural JPY selling has kept FX supply-demand balance skewed toward excess JPY supply. Second, the market's assessment of the policy mix—overly accommodative monetary policy and fiscal risks (particularly under the Takaichi administration)—has weighed on the currency. However, structural JPY selling is now moderating. On a basic balance basis (current account plus direct investment), Japan has moved into surplus, while the euro area surplus has narrowed and the US continues to run a sizable deficit. Japanese equities' outperformance versus US and European peers also points to an improvement in JPY fundamentals.

# When higher yields become supportive

Since 2024, JPY has weakened despite a widening disconnect from rate differentials. However, with improving JPY flow dynamics, a narrowing bank loan-deposit gap, and rising real rates, further increases in yen rates may begin to act as a supportive factor for the currency if fiscal risk peaks.

# Three JPY depreciation risks and the risk-reward balance

Even as structural JPY selling fades, several factors remain downside risks for the currency: (1) concerns about Bank of Japan (BoJ) independence and the risk of the BoJ being behind the curve, (2) fiscal risks, and (3) higher oil prices. However, these risks are not unique to JPY. Taking into account the likelihood of policy responses and valuation, we assess the overall risk balance as more neutral.

# Market implications

1) Reconsider the “sell JPY strength” strategy; 2) reassess hedging strategies; 3) reassess JPY as a funding currency; and 4) nonetheless, a clear JPY long requires catalysts (10Y yields at 3%, USD/JPY at 160, crude oil below US\$90/barrel).

# Risks

A slowdown in AI-related exports, persistently high oil prices, an acceleration in household JPY selling, and an expansion of monetary and fiscal risks.

# JP rates: Fiscal policy back in focus

# (Tomonobu Yamashita)

# Key theme: JGB selling fueled by supply-demand imbalance and budget risks

Yen rates have risen significantly, with the 10-year JGB yield rising above 2.7% and the 30-year reaching a record-high level. While net JGB supply is widely expected to increase in 2026 versus 2025, the May auction schedule and supplementary budget headlines are fueling selling pressure (see Liquid Insight: Global government bond supply update: same same, but different 14 May 2026).

Since late-April to early May was Japan's national holiday period, the JGB auction schedule has been particularly crowded from mid-May through early June. Meanwhile, demand for JGBs has been subdued, except from Japanese pension funds. As a result, each auction is adding to selling pressure on JGBs.

In addition, media reports suggest the Japanese government is considering a first supplementary budget for FY2026 to finance energy subsidies for households. Even if the Takaichi administration decides to compile the supplementary budget by the end of the current Diet session, which runs through 17 July, we do not expect a near-term increase in auction sizes. However, this could act as an obstacle to buying JGBs on dips (see Japan Viewpoint: Cost of energy subsidies: rising risk of a supplementary budget ahead of summer 23 April 2026).

# Forecast: We raise our end-2026 10-year forecast to 2.9%

Overall, we expect these supply-demand imbalances to persist at least until early June. Hence, we raise our end-2026 forecast for the 10-year JGB yield from 2.6% to 2.9%.

# Risk: Equity prices drop

Japanese pension funds' portfolio rebalancing—selling equities and buying domestic bonds—provides some support to the JGB market. However, a potential decline in equity markets could instead add to upward pressure on JGB yields.

# Banks (Shinichiro Nakamura / Yuji Kobayashi)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

Initial FY3/27 NP plans imply YoY growth of c.5-25% for the major banks and c.10-30% for regional banks. Given assumptions of no / one BoJ rate hike, the Nikkei 225 at ¥55,000, and USD/JPY at ¥150, we believe these plans have an upside bias (Exhibit 53/Exhibit 54). Going forward, we think expectations for outperformance should increase, particularly for the major banks.

MUFG: We view the initial plan as realistic, assuming one BoJ rate hike and credit costs, but consolidated NP is 10% above consensus, which is somewhat positive. Assumptions for the Nikkei 225 at ¥55,000 & equity-related gains/losses look somewhat conservative.

Mizuho: Core gross business profit is 5% above our forecast, based on assumptions of no BoJ rate hike, the Nikkei 225 at ¥57,000, and USD/JPY at ¥150, while consolidated NP is in line with our expectation. We see scope for an upward revision at the 1H results.

SMFG: Although consolidated NP is slightly below our forecast / consensus, we view the plan as somewhat conservative, given assumptions of no BoJ rate hike, USD/JPY at ¥150, and a decline in equity gains. We see a high likelihood of upward revision at 1H results.

Resona: Consolidated NP is above consensus even under conservative BoJ rate hike assumptions, and credit costs / equity-related gains and losses also look conservative. We believe there is a high likelihood of an upward revision at the 1H results.

For regional banks, initial plans are stronger than we expected, supported by higher TIBOR and improving loan spreads. We see significant scope for upward revisions at Yokohama, Chiba, Shizuoka, Fukuoka, and Mebuki, whose plans assume a policy rate of 0.75%. At the 1Q results, there remains scope for Shizuoka, Mebuki, Gunma, and Daishi Hokuetsu, which refrained from announcing share buybacks at the FY3/26 results, to announce buybacks. Chiba could also announce an additional buyback at the 1Q results once its existing program is completed.

# Key catalysts / events

We remain medium-term bullish on Japanese bank stocks and regard the major banks as attractive, for three reasons: (1) although Middle East geopolitical risks persist, the probability of a BoJ rate hike at the June-July meeting is high; (2) FY3/27 NP plans are above market expectations, and expectations for upward revisions at the 1H results are also high; and (3) on FY3/28 ROE, ROE premium vs TOPIX should widen further, while investors' focus should increasingly shift to FY3/28 from the FY3/27 1H results onward.

# Recommended stocks

MUFG (8306): (1) MUFG has suggested a direction of aiming for medium- to long-term ROE of 14% (assuming a policy rate of 1.25% or higher) and PBR of over 2x, which we expect to be achievable under the next medium-term management plan through FY3/30; (2) the FY3/27 NP plan of ¥2.7tn is 10% above consensus, and its DPS forecast of ¥96 offers some investment appeal; and (3) we expect additional share buybacks to be expanded at the 1H results, as the CET1 ratio is likely to return to the lower end of the target range (Mitsubishi UFJ Financial Group (8306): FY3/26 briefing: next MTP suggests ROE 14%; buyback boost at 1H results expected 19 May 2026).

Mizuho (8411): (1) The FY3/27 NP plan of ¥1.3tn (EPS of ¥534) has upside mainly from market environment assumptions, as it assumes no BoJ rate hike; (2) the FY3/29 ROE target could be around 13.5% assuming a BoJ policy rate of 1.25%, broadly in line with MUFG's level and solid; and (3) additional share buybacks will be decided after examining future loan demand, while management clarified that the Rakuten Bank investment is not a constraint, suggesting around ¥300bn could be possible (Mizuho Financial Group (8411): FY3/26 briefing: Rakuten Bank investment does not appear to pose buyback downside 19 May 2026).

Resona (8308): (1) The FY3/29 NP target implies ROE of 14% when the policy rate is 1.5% (our forecast is around 15%), and fee income should expand from FY3/28 onward; (2) the inorganic investment budget of ¥200bn is three times the previous medium-term plan, and the company plans to consider a wide range of opportunities, including regional bank M&A as well as securities / nonbank businesses; and (3) Resona still has a relative advantage, with loan spreads improving in lending yields and deposit beta controlled relative to peers in deposit yields (Resona Holdings (8308): FY3/26 briefing: ROE 14% envisaged at 1.5% policy rate; focus also on M&A 19 May 2026).

BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH

# Insurance, non-banks, and brokerage (Natsumu Tsujino)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

Securities: In the short term, we pay the most attention to this sector, alongside life insurers. Equity market conditions remain strong, with trading value continuing to increase, and inflows into face-to-face brokerage channels also ongoing. While earnings dependence on market conditions makes long-term investment less straightforward, for now profits are likely to be sustained at levels that imply dividend yields above 4%. For long-term holdings, we believe a close monitoring of sudden market changes is required.

Life insurers: We pay more attention to life insurers over non-life in the short term, but regard non-life as more attractive than life insurers in the long term. Inflation impacts have already been visible in auto insurance for non-life, but life insurers are also facing rising expenses, expiry of older high-margin policies, and declining in-force contracts, leading to weaker insurance-related profits. Non-life insurers benefit from shorter liabilities, greater pricing flexibility, and demand that is increasingly driven by inflation and rising perils, while interest in protection-type life insurance is moderating. That said, in the short term, investment income is supporting earnings recovery. Many life insurers are delivering DPS growth above 10%, with dividend yields approaching the 4% range, higher than for non-life, making them more attractive in the near term.

Non-life insurers: Share-price momentum may be weak in the short term, as expectations for improved shareholder returns had become overly elevated. Among the group, MS&AD offers relatively high dividend and total shareholder-return yields, making it attractive. In contrast, for Tokio Marine HD and Sompo HD, which are seen as pursuing large-scale M&A, dividend growth remains strong but share buybacks are now expected to be smaller than anticipated. Share prices may lack direction until more clarity emerges on likely large M&A. Within non-life, the loss ratio for domestic auto insurance is expected to improve in FY3/27. This is the first time since deterioration began that management guidance points to improvement from the start of fiscal year, suggesting that pricing increases are finally taking effect—positive for sector momentum.

Other financials: ORIX is in a unique situation. With Toshiba shifting its accounting for Kioxia shares to FVTPL, ORIX's equity-method earnings will become significantly more volatile. A ¥10,000 increase in Kioxia's share price would contribute approximately 1.0% of ORIX's current market capitalization through equity-method earnings from Toshiba. While strong momentum in Kioxia shares is supporting ORIX's share price, this appears somewhat excessive. The stock remains prone to short-term volatility and higher risk. Among other non-banks, situations are more idiosyncratic; Credit Saison looks increasingly undervalued, while Mitsubishi HC Capital still appears the most expensive.

# Key catalysts / events

Higher 1Q progress rates are likely for Credit Saison, Japan Exchange Group (JPX)—as company assumptions for Tokyo Stock Exchange trading value remain overly conservative at FY3/26 levels—and the three non-life insurers (1Q typically sees fewer natural disasters, although actual outcomes remain uncertain). Life insurers usually report slightly weak 1Q earnings, as Japanese equity dividends are booked in 4Q.

Announcements of large-scale M&A could act as catalysts for Tokio Marine HD and Sompo HD.

# Recommended stocks

Dai-ichi Life Group (8750): Near-term earnings are supported by gains on equity sales, and proceeds are being used to pursue growth through M&A. While the business portfolio several years out is difficult to foresee, the relatively high dividend yield and management's dividend-focused stance suggest strong investment appeal.

Sompo HD (8630): Management plans to raise the payout ratio based on 3-year average adjusted profit to 50% by FY3/31 (39% in FY3/27). Although the share price declined post-results on perceptions of limited appetite for share buybacks, this policy implies DPS can grow at around a 20% annual rate over the next 3–4 years. In addition, the company retains capacity to generate ¥100bn (vs. FY3/27 Co.E adj. profit of ¥500bn) in incremental profit through M&A. Despite a currently low dividend yield, the investment appeal remains strong.

Daiwa Group (8601): Our estimates do not include the impact of the ORIX Bank acquisition, which is likely to be closed in Oct. Even after goodwill amortization, we estimate a 7–8% uplift to full-year earnings. While significant synergies are unlikely in the short term, if current market conditions persist, dividend yields in the low-to-mid 4% range are achievable, suggesting relatively strong investment appeal, albeit with attention to market conditions.

# Semiconductors/SPE (Mikio Hirakawa/Mayako Ouchi)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

AI and data centers are expected to remain key themes in the April–June quarter.

However, we believe the semiconductor and semiconductor production equipment (SPE) front-end cycle, which has been driven by memory pricing, is moving into its later phase. In stock selection, having clear catalysts will be an important requirement.

# Key catalysts / events

We focus on Renesas's Capital Markets Day (25 June).

Renesas commented at its October–December quarter earnings call that it will redefine its data center-related business and discuss the opportunity at its Capital Markets Day. We believe this enhanced disclosure could lead to the market assigning an AI premium to Renesas's valuation.

# Recommended stock

Our Buy thesis on Renesas is based on 1) growth in data center-related revenue and 2) recovery in the automotive semiconductor market. In its Jan–Mar quarter results, Renesas' 2Q gross margin guidance fell short of our/market expectations due to higher manufacturing costs, which was a negative. But, recovery trends were confirmed in both automotive and non-automotive markets, and the company stated that demand from the Apr–Jun quarter onward will be stronger than initially expected. As noted above, we see the key catalyst as discussion of the data center business at the Capital Markets Day.

# Electronics (Mikio Hirakawa)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

We are positive on the electrical equipment sector for the April–June quarter. Among industrial electronics companies, infrastructure-related businesses will act as key drivers, while in consumer electronics, Panasonic (data center-related) and Sony (gaming-related) are each on an earnings growth trend, driven by their respective segments.

# Catalysts and events

Four companies will host capital markets events in the April–June quarter: Panasonic, Hitachi, Mitsubishi Electric, and Fuji Electric.

At Panasonic's Investor Day (8 June), the company will focus on explaining its Energy and Industry segment strategies. In particular, the revenue expansion strategy for AI-related businesses within the Industry segment will be a key point of interest. At its FY3/26 full-year earnings briefing, the company outlined a plan to expand Industry AI-related revenue (under a new definition) from ¥230bn in FY3/26 to ¥430bn in FY3/29. If Investor Day provides a convincing strategy for this expansion, it could lead to a re-rating of Panasonic's valuation.

At Mitsubishi Electric's IR Day (29 May), the focus will be on balance sheet (capital) efficiency and digitalization. Regarding balance-sheet efficiency, if the company presents targets exceeding previously reported figures—such as an FY3/28 ROE of 10% and a total payout ratio of over 70% during the new medium-term plan period (vs. the current target of over 50%)—the stock price is likely to react positively. On digitalization, strong execution of a strategy leveraging Nozomi Networks, an OT (operational technology) security solutions company whose acquisition is set to be completed in February 2026, could have a positive impact on valuation.

At Hitachi's Investor Day (10 June), the key focus will be its physical AI strategy. In the medium-term plan update announced alongside its FY3/26 results, Hitachi disclosed that HMAX, a recurring digital service positioned as a driver of physical AI, generated ¥300bn in revenue with an EBITA margin above 20% in FY3/26. If Hitachi can clearly explain 1) the project composition of HMAX revenue by segment, 2) the visibility and monetization strategy for HMAX opportunities in each segment, and 3) its medium- to long-term revenue and margin targets for HMAX, this could lead to an improved valuation.

At Fuji Electric's business strategy briefing (27 May), the focus will be on its medium-term target payout ratio of $50\%$ (reached through share buybacks in FY3/27), its overseas strategy in the energy segment (its main growth driver), and its strategy for the underperforming automotive semiconductor business.

# Recommended stock

Our Buy thesis for Panasonic is based on 1) growth in data center BBU (battery backup unit) demand and 2) improved and stabilized profitability driven by structural reforms. At the 8 June Investor Day, we will focus on the competitive environment and order progress in data center BBUs, as well as growth strategies for AI-related businesses in the Industry segment. We also believe that the visible impact of structural reforms implemented in FY3/26, leading to improved earnings stability, will serve as a catalyst for share-price upside.

# Electronic components sector (Masashi Kubota)

# Sector view for next quarter

In the electronic components sector heading into the Jul-Sep 2026, positive factors include (1) continued expansion in AI server and data center-related business, (2) demand growth driven by iPhone seasonality, and (3) stable product price trends supported by tight supply demand balance. On the other hand, points to monitor include (1) the impact of rising material prices such as gold and copper, (2) the impact of higher crude oil prices associated with worsening Middle East conditions, and (3) the risk of slowing end-demand for automobiles and IT devices.

However, regarding rising material prices, companies are steadily passing on costs to product prices. In addition, while there is a risk of demand slowdown for automobiles and IT devices, there is a growing view that the negative impact will not be as significant as previously feared, supported by progress in electrification and firm demand outlook for high-end smartphones.

# Catalysts and events

On the other hand, it is true that expectations for earnings growth driven by expansion in AI-related business have already been largely priced into share prices, and valuation discounts of electronic component manufacturers have diminished. Therefore, for share prices to show further upside, events that push up expectations for earnings will be required. Specifically, these could include (1) product price increases backed by tighter supply demand balance and (2) demand for IT devices such as smartphones, PCs, and tablets exceeding prior assumptions of a slowdown.

In AI-related areas, in addition to increased investment in traditional AI servers, attention should also be paid to the potential acceleration in expansion of the edge AI market represented by physical AI.

# Key area to watch

Within the electronic components sector, we continue to focus on AI-related areas, including semiconductor packaging, passive components (MLCCs, inductors, aluminum electrolytic capacitors, sensors), and HDD-related electronic components (magnetic heads, suspensions, glass substrates). Supported by strong demand, we think tightening supply-demand balance and the resulting outlook for stable pricing could drive a significant improvement in profitability for related companies.

# Internet, game, and media (Yoshitaka Nagao)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

This earnings season highlighted a tug-of-war between upfront investment for the medium term and the visibility of earnings growth. We saw improving earnings visibility at several companies, including Recruit, where the HR Tech segment guided for +18% YoY pricing, PayPay, where operating leverage is beginning to emerge, and CyberAgent, where all three businesses performed well simultaneously. On the other hand, uncertainty around cost plans became a common overhang for FY3/27, with higher logistics costs at ZOZO, and continued upfront investment in Kakaku.com's Kyujin Box.

In the game sector, the contrast between the earnings power of core IP and conservative FY3/27 guidance stood out. Konami's eFootball remains strong, and Capcom's Resident Evil series reached 6.91mn units, underscoring resilient IP-driven monetization. However, company guidance for FY3/27 was broadly conservative and below market expectations, including Bandai Namco guiding for OP decline, Capcom remaining cautious, Konami falling short of expectations, and Square Enix also guiding for OP decline. We think this conservatism in guidance weighed on share prices.

# Key catalysts / events

In the media sector, the key theme was cautious topline assumptions alongside improvements in cost control and capital policy. Dentsu G saw its domestic OP margin improve by 1.8ppt YoY, while Hakuhodo DY cut SG&A by 0.2% for the full year, resulting in OP growth of 18.9% YoY, indicating disciplined cost management at both companies. In contrast, the four broadcasters guided for OP decline in FY3/27, reflecting conservative assumptions for spot advertising. We think the evaluation focus remains on the balance sheet. TV Asahi HD raised its payout ratio from 20% to 40%, Nippon TV HD announced a ¥12bn share buyback, and TBS HD indicated cumulative shareholder returns of ¥105bn over three years, highlighting a clearer commitment to shareholder returns. Meanwhile, Fuji Media HD delivered a P/L surprise, with OP plan of ¥40.1bn significantly exceeding market expectations, confirming a return to an earnings recovery trajectory.

# Recommended stocks)

Across the three sectors, we summarize the FY3/26 results with Recruit HD (6098), Capcom (9697), and CyberAgent (4751) as names where we maintain Buy rating. Recruit benefits from AI-driven pricing expansion in HR Tech, Capcom continues to demonstrate the earning power of its core IP, and CyberAgent sees strong performance across all three businesses. We think all three have further improved earnings visibility. Nintendo's (7974) medium-term growth story also remains intact, supported by pricing power for Switch 2 and a broader software lineup. On the other hand, we flag Bandai Namco HD and Nexon as names to watch. Bandai Namco (7832) has a back-end loaded earnings profile, and Nexon (3659) shows signs of slowing in core titles.

# IT service sector (Hideaki Tanaka)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

We expect earnings to remain firm. While share prices of major system integrators have shown signs of stabilizing following the US Anthropic shock, a full recovery has yet to materialize. Looking ahead, we believe share prices will rise gradually, supported by sustained strong earnings and clearer demonstrations of AI adoption and its benefits. However, we do not expect a sharp rebound. Rather than a self-driven recovery, we believe inflows into the sector will be driven by a shift in risk-off asset allocation.

Over the medium term, we expect the equity market to increasingly recognize AI as a net positive for earnings, particularly among large system integrators, paving the way for a more meaningful recovery in share prices (see our report dated 15 April).

Review of FY1–3/26 results: While NOM Institute (4307, NRI) revised down FY3/26 earnings significantly owing to a ¥96.9bn impairment loss in its overseas business, overall earnings were generally solid excluding this factor. Profitability trends remain positive, with Fujitsu (6702) and NEC (6701) showing notable margin improvements in IT services, supported by strong offering services. On the other hand, although FY3/27 guidance was broadly in line with consensus, even minor downside deviations were negatively received by the market.

Outlook for FY4–6/26: We expect major system integrators such as NEC, Fujitsu, and NRI to deliver strong earnings, with double-digit operating profit growth. We also forecast solid performance from package and cloud-based vendors, including OBIC (4684) and OBC (4733).

# Key catalysts / events

Previously expected catalysts—the new medium-term management plans (MTPs) from NEC, Fujitsu, and NRI—did not drive share-price performance.

NEC's five-year MTP was positive, targeting a profit CAGR of $15\%$ (see our note dated 12 May). However, despite FY3/27 guidance slightly missing consensus due to incorporating procurement risks, the share-price reaction was limited on the day following the announcement. NRI's plan was received negatively, reflecting conservative assumptions on margin improvement.

Fujitsu is scheduled to announce its new MTP on 28 May. While the format of its financial targets remains unclear, if it presents five-year targets, the key focus will be on the extent to which it can exceed a $10\%$ CAGR. In the near term, sustained strong earnings performance is likely to be the main share-price driver. In terms of events, in addition to Fujitsu's MTP announcement, NEC is scheduled to hold an IR Day on 1 June. We believe management's continued emphasis on AI adoption and its positive impact could prompt a reassessment of earnings sustainability.

# Recommended stocks

We continue to focus on major system integrators, including NEC, Fujitsu, and NRI. Among these, we highlight NEC in particular, as we forecast a top-tier Non-GAAP operating profit CAGR of 17% over the next three years, leading the sector.

BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH

# Auto, auto parts and tire (Shiro Sakamaki)

# Sector view for the next quarter

In the near term, positives and negatives are likely to be mixed across OEMs. At the sector level, downside risk remains from production cuts due to naphtha shortages not factored into company guidance. As a result, companies may present conservative plans, while Toyota Motor (7203), with relatively strong procurement capabilities, should offer reassurance. That said, even for Toyota, some caution is warranted around potential share sales by financial institutions and the scope for consensus estimate cuts. Subaru Corp (7270) also appears to have set a fairly conservative plan, but upside to earnings will likely require solid US monthly sales data. Meanwhile, Mitsubishi Motors (7211), with high exposure to Asia where macro concerns linked to higher oil prices tend to weigh, and Suzuki Motor (7269), where production in India remains a concern following Prime Minister Narendra Modi's 10 May call to limit outings, could see share-price rebounds if Middle East tensions ease. For Mitsubishi Motors in particular, while its plan is not clearly conservative, expectations for its medium-term plan announcement, including alliance developments, should support the share price in the near term. Among companies that presented relatively strong full-year plans, Nissan Motor (7201) could see its 1Q progress appear modest. However, with the 1 July revision of the USMCA (United States–Mexico–Canada Agreement) approaching, both Nissan and Mazda Motor (7261) should benefit if the Mexican government succeeds in securing lower tariffs on exports to the US. Honda Motor (7267), which suggested a shift away from a self-reliant approach, now faces scrutiny over how it will fill the gap from cancelling BEV launches in North America next year, and clearer details on its forward strategy, including implications for affiliated suppliers, are awaited.

Among suppliers, attention is likely to focus on Toyota-affiliated names, which should benefit from relatively stable production volumes given the supply backdrop outlined above. Koito Manufacturing (7276) should continue to see share-price support from expectations for narrowing losses in its LiDAR business and a potential share buyback announcement. Meanwhile, for DENSO (6902), the next strategic step following the ROHM acquisition is awaited. For Honda-affiliated suppliers, the impact of Honda's evolving procurement strategy warrants close monitoring. Companies with growth drivers outside the automotive sector, such as NHK Spring (5991), are also likely to attract attention. In particular, NHK Spring offers upside not only from its HDD suspension business but also from semiconductor process components, for which it is targeting around 50% revenue growth.

In tires, there is concern about potential guidance cuts by companies such as Sumitomo Rubber (5110), which implemented price adjustments in the US from April. However, if Middle East tensions ease, a pullback in commodity prices, including natural rubber and crude oil, could present a buying opportunity. On a company-specific basis, expectations for a share buyback at Toyo Tire (5105) remain.

# Catalysts and events

As noted, developments in the Middle East are the key macro event. At the company level, focus will also be on Mitsubishi Motors' medium-term plan announcement around end-May, and developments in auto tariffs stemming from the USMCA review. As Denso concludes its share-buyback program into the summer, its next strategic move will be closely watched. Meanwhile, in tires, industry consolidation will be a key area of focus toward the second half.

# Recommended stocks

With visibility on earnings outlook still limited, Honda Motor may remain relatively resilient, supported by factors such as its dividend yield alongside expectations for the next step in structural reform. Mitsubishi Motors also warrants attention on its medium-term plan. Among suppliers, amid some lingering uncertainty over production volume outlooks, NHK Spring appears relatively undervalued considering Seagate's share-price strength in the HDD industry and remains a focus name, alongside Koito Manufacturing.

# Machinery (Kenjin Hotta)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

Among the key subsectors under our coverage, we remain relatively positive on factory automation (FA). Total FA-related orders rose 19% QoQ in 1Q CY2026, providing a solid set-up for 2Q CY2026. Moreover, based on our company discussions, we get the sense that order trends remain firm, likely supporting earnings momentum into 3Q CY2026.

We also believe that current semiconductor-related order run rates imply around mid-teens growth in FA demand in FY3/27, and even in the absence of a broad-based recovery, growth in FY3/27 should remain solid.

By contrast, we remain relatively cautious on construction and mining equipment, which we believe lacks a strong near-term volume growth driver, although we do not see evidence of any deterioration in underlying trends.

Finally, we take a neutral view on the heavy industrials, reflecting a lack of clear catalysts, and in the case of IHI, likely headwinds from weaker commercial aero engine-related demand.

# Key catalysts / events

The potential announcement of the next defense spending plan in June, though recent reporting suggests potential delays in publishing an actual target for spending.

Ongoing monitoring of semis related data points (e.g. South Korea semis exports and Chipmaking equipment billings)

# Recommended stock

Among our heavy industries coverage, our recommended stock is KHI (7012). With the Powersports & Engine business no longer expected to be a major earnings headwind, we believe FY3/27 should mark an earnings inflection point. In the meantime, valuations remain relatively attractive, in our view.

Within our construction-related coverage, we highlight Makita (6586). While volume growth in FY3/27 is likely to remain modest, we expect valuations to benefit from management's increased focus on improving ROE.

# Transport (Nathan Gee / Hiro Nakakura)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

Shipping: Container shipping fundamentals tightening in the near term. But Iran war impacts are far more mixed than COVID and the Red Sea, and oversupply pressures looming into 2027-28. Watch scope for shareholder-return surprises from NYK and K-Line once Iran war uncertainties ease.

Airlines: Iran war a clear negative with a sharp earnings contraction expected in June'26 quarter. But Japanese airlines look better cushioned given fuel subsidies, hedging, tight international passenger market and robust cargo pricing. Domestic supply reforms could offer upside to medium-term profits.

Railways: Focus is shifting toward non-transport businesses (primarily real estate), as no major updates on railway pricing is expected. Cross-shareholdings reduction is likely to continue and could lead to additional shareholder-return surprise in 2H.

Trucking: Delivery volume is expected to grow, supported by both domestic and cross-border EC demand. The key focus will be on managing the volume mix and sustaining improvements in unit pricing. Earnings growth should continue to be driven by cost control, given limited visibility for major price hikes in the near term.

# Key catalysts/ events

Iran war: Iran war having a big impact on shipping and airlines – so all eyes on potential resolution, including possible reopening of Hormuz.

Inbound tourism: A recovery in Chinese inbound demand is unlikely in the near term. However, if inbound traffic from North America and Europe softens, it could weigh on rail and hotel segment profitability, posing downside risk.

# Recommended stocks

ANA (9202): Valuation near the bottom at 1x PBV. Upside risks around domestic supply reforms and strong cargo and international pricing power, while support from large buyback ongoing.

Odakyu (9007): Earnings visibility has improved further following the recent plan update. Combined with an uplift in shareholder returns and additional return measures, Odakyu offers one of the highest total shareholders returns among peers, while still retaining further upside to shareholder returns over the coming years.

# Electronic materials sector (Niima Komura)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

Driven by robust AI demand, we expect 1Q FY2026 earnings for semiconductor material names to show solid progress, with upside increasingly coming into focus. Many companies may not have fully incorporated the impact of agentic AI adoption into their guidance, and we believe valuations remain attractive even for those that have issued bullish outlooks.

In AI server-related segments, stronger demand for 1.6T transceivers and the ramp-up to mass production of ceramic heat dissipation substrates should support a further step-up in MARUWA's earnings.

# Key catalysts / events

Adoption of CMP slurry in advanced packaging by manufacturers would expand Fuso Chemical's exposure to back-end processes via its customers, supporting a potential re-rating of its valuation.

In addition, confirmation of (1) upward revisions to earnings outlook by foundries and memory manufacturers, (2) increased AI capex by hyperscalers in 2027, and (3) broader adoption of agentic AI could serve as positive catalysts for semiconductor materials and AI component names.

# Recommended stocks

Tokyo Ohka Kogyo (4186) reported 1Q results, suggesting it is on track to achieve its revised medium-term OP target of ¥58bn for FY12/27 as early as this fiscal year, driven by robust AI demand. We expect an upward revision as early as 2Q, while the market is likely to start pricing in a meaningful upside to the medium-term plan beyond that.

Fuso Chemical (4368), despite issuing unusually bullish guidance for FY3/27, may not have fully incorporated the impact of agentic AI adoption into its company plan. As such, we believe 1Q results should pave the way for upward revisions.

MARUWA (5344) has set full-year guidance that appears overly conservative, reflecting geopolitical risks. However, we see significant upside potential in its AI and semiconductor businesses, and expect internal forecasts to be raised following 1Q results. Meanwhile, the market is likely to anticipate further upside, supporting a continued upward trend in the share price. In addition, any pull-forward in shipments of ceramic heat dissipation substrates for CPO, currently scheduled for 2H, could provide an additional upside driver for its AI-related business.

# Glass & ceramic / paper & pulp (Kyoji Chiba)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

In the glass & ceramics sector, we continue to see AI-related growth as the key driver. We focus on catalysts tied to AI semiconductors, including Nichias's (5393) materials for semiconductor production equipment (SPE), Nippon Electric Glass's (5214) semiconductor support glass, AGC's (5201) extreme ultraviolet (EUV) mask blanks and CCL. In the cement sector, investor perception of Sumitomo Osaka Cement (5232), which develops electrostatic chucks, could change.

In the paper & pulp sector, we see the impact of rising logistics and labor costs, as well as higher raw material and fuel prices driven by Middle East developments. In Japan, ongoing price increases are likely insufficient to offset cost pressures, and further hikes will be needed. We also see expectations for industry restructuring, and will closely monitor developments at each company.

# Key catalysts / events

AGC plans to hold a semiconductor business briefing on 2 June, which could lead to renewed recognition of its AI exposure. Positive AI-related news flow should support valuations of the two glass companies and SPE-related names (Nichias and Sumitomo Osaka Cement). For the three paper & pulp companies, we look for announcements of additional domestic price increases.

# Recommended stocks

Nichias: We expect growth in materials for SPE and increased maintenance demand following nuclear power plant restarts. The company also has scope to enhance shareholder returns backed by strong cash inflows.

Nippon Electric Glass: Although the share price declined after 1Q FY12/26 results, we believe its AI-related growth story remains intact. We also watch for announcements related to advanced packaging materials such as low thermal expansion glass fiber and glass core substrates.

AGC: Following the above briefing, the market could again recognize the company's AI-related growth potential.

# Retail sector (Arashi Nishizawa)

# Sector view for the next quarter

Key points from the earnings announcements in Apr-May centered on the impact of Middle East tensions. At present, aside from some signs of rush demand for items such as toilet paper, there has been no major impact. However, with utility costs rising and cost inflation persisting, the focus is on the extent to which this will affect consumption. Given rising labor costs as well, companies will likely have no choice but to pass on costs. That said, we expect some companies to respond to changes in consumer behavior by seeking to secure gross profit dollars rather than gross margin.

At present, there are concerns about the impact on domestic consumption and cost inflation for daily necessities. However, if Middle East tensions become prolonged, the focus is likely to shift toward the impact on inbound demand, as well as pricing power of companies such as apparel retailers, where cost hikes tend to emerge with a time lag.

# Catalysts and events

If the challenging environment is expected to continue, we believe this could serve as a catalyst for further industry consolidation. We have been focusing on consolidation in the food supermarket sector, and there have also been developments such as PPIH (7532) acquiring Olympics. We also get the impression that more companies are considering M&A as an option to secure store locations. We expect competition to intensify across industry boundaries, including among discounters and drugstores.

# Recommended stocks

We continue to focus on Fast Retailing (9983), ASICS (7936), and Ryohin Keikaku (7453), which have growth drivers overseas. In terms of capturing domestic demand, we focus on PPIH (7532), Trial (141A), Kobe Bussan (3038), which we believe are likely to expand share while controlling margins.

# Food & beverage (Manabu Sumoge)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

Results for the January–March 2026 period were generally solid. However, across both December fiscal year-end companies in beer and soft drinks and March fiscal year-end companies in processed foods, discussion centered on cost inflation driven by the Middle East situation and the corresponding countermeasures. Companies providing insufficient explanation appeared to underperform in share price terms. Cost inflation related to the Middle East is expected to materialize mainly in the second half of the year, requiring responses that extend into FY3/27. At the same time, the magnitude of the impact remains uncertain, and many companies have not incorporated this into their plans, representing downside risk to earnings forecasts. Against this backdrop, we see three key focus areas for the sector: (1) clearer disclosure on the scale of cost increases linked to the Middle East situation; (2) the quality of pricing actions, in terms of both speed and magnitude, to offset such costs; and (3) visibility on earnings, including FY3/27. We believe companies that can respond swiftly to cost inflation while improving margins once the external environment stabilizes are likely to be relatively preferred.

Within sub-sectors, we regard beer and condiments as attractive. For beer, while competition is expected to intensify toward the liquor tax revision in October 2026, the industry's oligopolistic structure with four major names and ongoing fixed-cost optimization, driven by volume declines, suggest relatively high resilience to cost inflation. Condiments are supported by a high overseas sales ratio and the relatively limited volume impact following past price hikes. In contrast, while further price increases in soft drinks are likely, it will be important to assess whether companies can sufficiently absorb cost inflation given the current competitive environment.

# Key catalysts / events

Price hike announcements across industries; monthly domestic sales data for beer and soft drinks (June, early July); US Nielsen data.

# Recommended stocks

Kikkoman (2801): In 4Q FY3/26, North America sales growth (excluding FX) remained strong, with soy sauce at +7.6% and wholesale at +8.3%. By contrast, the company's assumptions for FY3/27 overseas sales growth (excluding FX) are +3.6% for North America soy sauce and +4.8% for wholesale, below the 4Q run rate. We view these assumptions as conservative, particularly in light of a recovery in exports to Canada. Costs may exceed plan, but we believe these can be addressed through cost reductions and pricing, as seen in 2022. Over the medium term, we expect improved sales growth driven by new plant operations and an acceleration in ROE improvement.

Kirin Holdings (2503): 1Q FY12/26 business profit significantly exceeded both our forecast and consensus, even excluding Kyowa Kirin. We maintain our business profit forecast to reflect potential incremental cost inflation arising from the Middle East situation. At the earnings briefing, management noted uncertainties in visibility but stated that if cost increases remain within a manageable range and are appropriately addressed, this could present upside. Our business profit forecast of ¥250.2bn is above company guidance and consensus, but considering the 1Q upside, along with ongoing cost reductions and potential additional price hikes, we believe it remains well within an achievable range.

Exhibit 77: Outperformance drivers include strong earnings, effective management of cost inflation stemming from the Middle East situation, and capital policy initiatives.

# Cosmetics & toiletries sector (Kentaro Hosoda)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

We see two key impacts from the Middle East situation: (1) supply constraints on oil-derived materials leading to production disruptions, and (2) higher raw material costs driven by rising oil prices. On (1), some companies are already seeing disruptions to new product launch cycles and shipments, and we think this will start to show more clearly in the Apr–Jun results. While firms are trying to manage this by production reallocation and delaying launches, we still need to look for risks if the situation persists. On (2), toiletry companies have been relatively proactive on price hikes, and given the essential nature of the products, the hurdle for passing on costs remains relatively low, which is somewhat reassuring. Based on the above, we summarize the sector fundamentals as balanced to slightly positive for toiletries, and negative for cosmetics.

# Key catalysts / events

Recently, we observed some rotation into defensive names, including toiletries, driven by rising JPY interest rates. Going forward, the key question is how much of the cost pressure from the Middle East situation can be offset through pricing. In the near term, we would screen names based on “valuation attractiveness” and “domestic demand plus margin recovery.”

# Recommended stocks

In a scenario where flows move into domestic defensives, we would focus on Lion (4912) given its relative valuation gap. Pigeon (7956) also looks attractive on a dividend yield basis, and if concerns around microplastics in the US continue to build and drive a shift toward glass baby bottles, there could be further upside. We also like Kao (4452) for its ability to maintain top-line growth even after passing through price increases.

# Biopharma sector (Koichi Mamegano)

# Sector overall view for the next quarter

Biopharma stocks have been re-rated since October 2025 owing to reduced uncertainty over the US Most Favored Nation (MFN) drug-pricing policy. However, momentum has recently softened. Looking at performance, only four stocks have outperformed TOPIX over the past 52 weeks. While the number of outperformers increased over the past six months, it has again declined to just four names over the most recent four-week period, in line with the 52-week trend, suggesting a loss of momentum at the sector level. Against the backdrop of a strong Japanese equity market, biopharma stocks are increasingly being positioned as relative defensives.

# Key catalysts / events

The US approval events to watch include Otsuka's centanafadine (ADHD), with a PDUFA date of 24 July; Takeda's oveporexton (narcolepsy type 1), 10 August; rusfertide (polycythemia vera), 29 August; Eisai's Leqembi IQLIK (Alzheimer's disease), 24 August; and Daiichi Sankyo's I-DXd (small cell lung cancer), 10 October. All of these are key events that could contribute to earnings growth for the respective companies.

For upcoming conference presentations, Otsuka's Voyxact (IgA nephropathy) is expected to present interim Phase-3 eGFR data at the European Renal Association (ERA; 3–6 Jun), while Ono's ONO-2808 will present positive Phase-2 data in multiple system atrophy at the International Congress of Parkinson's Disease and Movement Disorders (27 May), and ONO-4578 will present positive Phase-2 data in gastric cancer at ASCO (1 Jun). In evaluating these data, comparisons with competitor benchmarks will be important. In addition, topline Phase-2 readouts for multiple indications are expected for Ono's ONO-1110 (for depression and anxiety disorders), representing another key catalyst.

On strategic partnerships, Nxera's NXE'744 (EP4 receptor agonist) and NXE'149 (GPR52 agonist) are potential candidates for external partnering, with a possible timeline as early as mid-2026. Strategic investments to support medium- to long-term growth could also be undertaken by companies such as Otsuka, Shionogi, Santen, Eisai, and Kyowa Kirin, while Daiichi Sankyo is expected to continue divesting non-core assets.

Regarding corporate events, Ono plans to host an R&D Day on 8 June. Although timing has not yet been finalized, Shionogi is also expected to hold an R&D briefing, and Takeda plans to host a Capital Markets Day in the second half of FY3/27.

# Recommended stocks

From June, we highlight Otsuka (4578), Astellas (4503), and Takeda (4502) as key stocks of interest.

For Otsuka, the impact of patent expiries for Jynarque (ADPKD) has remained more limited than initially expected, raising expectations for a potential upward revision to full-year guidance heading into the 2Q results. Otsuka also has multiple near-term catalysts, including the presentation of interim Phase-3 eGFR data for Voyxact (IgA nephropathy) at the European Renal Association (ERA) in early June, and the anticipated US approval of centanafadine (ADHD) in late-July.

Astellas is expected to outline its post-Xtandi (prostate cancer) patent expiry growth strategy in its Corporate Strategic Plan 2026. The company aims to improve its core operating profit margin through growth in strategic products and cost optimization, while accelerating the development of internally discovered assets.

Takeda, in addition to its existing business, is slated to receive the US approvals in August for oveporexton (for narcolepsy type 1) and rusfertide (for polycythemia vera), which are expected to serve as key growth drivers going forward.

# Acronyms:

ADHD: Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder

ASCO: American Society of Clinical Oncology

IgA: Immunoglobulin A

eGFR: estimated Glomerular Filtration Rate

ERA: European Renal Association

GPR52: G protein-coupled receptor 52
